People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the main thing is... I just can’t keep my mind on a task long enough to actually finish it anymore, even when it’s something important. I work in computer support, so a lot of my job is following a problem step by step, like tracing a client’s network issue through logs or settings or whatever, and I’ll sit down intending to do that, but ten minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same log entries, or rereading the same few lines over and over, and I still couldn’t tell you what they actually say.

It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid it. I know it matters, and I’ll be looking right at the screen, but my mind just won’t stay with it long enough to get to the end. That’s really what brought me in.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I did. I mean, at the time I don’t think anybody called it “focus” exactly, but looking back it was definitely there. My mother still jokes about it, actually — she’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” and then the rest was that I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers before I was supposed to, and I was always losing homework.

And the annoying thing is, sometimes I had actually done the homework. It wasn’t always that I blew it off — I’d do it and then somehow it just wouldn’t make it to school, or I’d misplace it, or forget to turn it in. My mom still brings that up like it’s a funny family story, but now when I think about it, it feels a little less funny and more like, oh… this has been a pattern for a long time.

It wasn’t always that I blew it off — I’d do it and then somehow it just wouldn’t make it to school, or I’d misplace it, or forget to turn it in. My mom still brings that up like it’s a funny family story, but now when I think about it, it feels a little less funny and more like, oh… this has been a pattern for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I worry after I’ve already lost focus, if that makes sense — like I’ll realize I’ve wasted time or I’m not getting anywhere, and then I start getting tense about it. But I don’t know that worry is what starts it.

A lot of the time it feels more like my attention just sort of slips away without me deciding anything. I’ll be trying to read something or follow a thread of information, and then suddenly I’m somewhere else mentally. Not necessarily worrying about a specific thing, just… not with the task anymore. Then once I notice it, that’s when I get frustrated or start thinking about the consequences.

So I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed worry being the main thing pulling me away. It’s more like the drifting happens first, and the worry comes afterward.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t really been paying close attention in a way where I could tell you, like, “Yes, it takes me exactly an hour,” or “I wake up three times.” I don’t think I’ve been tracking it that specifically.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean, it’s not just work. Work is where it’s most obvious because the consequences are right in front of me, but the same thing happens in other parts of my life too.

The main thing that brought me in is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on something long enough to actually finish it anymore, even when I know it matters. Like, at work I’ll start tracing a client’s network problem, and I’m looking through logs or trying to follow the sequence of what happened, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve read the same few entries over and over and I still don’t actually know what they said. It’s like my eyes moved across the words, but my brain didn’t stay there.

And outside of work it feels similar, just with different tasks. If I’m trying to get something done that takes sustained attention, I lose the thread. I’ll start with the intention of finishing it, and then I’m mentally gone, or I have to keep restarting because I didn’t absorb what I was doing. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. Sometimes it’s important, and I still can’t hold onto it long enough to get through it cleanly.

I work in computer support, so a lot of my job is following a problem step by step, like tracing a client’s network issue through logs or settings or whatever, and I’ll sit down intending to do that, but ten minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same log entries, or rereading the same few lines over and over, and I still couldn’t tell you what they actually say.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid it. I know it matters, and I’ll be looking right at the screen, but my mind just won’t stay with it long enough to get to the end. I’ll start with the intention of finishing it, and then I’m mentally gone, or I have to keep restarting because I didn’t absorb what I was doing. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. Sometimes it’s important, and I still can’t hold onto it long enough to get through it cleanly.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave these focus problems caused significant problems in your relationships?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 53 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"Since losing the structure of my old job, my reputation has taken a real hit—I’ve lost freelance clients, missed out on a senior service-manager role, and my wife and I have had to worry about money because people don’t trust me to follow through.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean, the most obvious damage has probably been professional, but it definitely spills into relationships, especially because people start to see you as unreliable.

Since I lost the structure of my old job, it’s like there isn’t that same external system holding me in place, and my reputation has taken a real hit. With freelance clients, if I don’t follow through cleanly or I miss things or I’m late getting back to them, they don’t really care what the internal reason is — they just stop trusting me. I’ve lost clients that way. And that’s hard not to take personally, because I know I’m capable, but from their side it just looks like I’m not dependable.

There was also a senior service-manager role I was trying for, and I missed out on that. That one really stung, because it felt like the kind of position I should have been able to step into at this stage of my career. But when your follow-through is inconsistent, people notice.

And at home, yeah, it affects my marriage. My wife and I have had to worry about money more than we should, because if clients don’t trust me to finish things or stay on top of things, the income gets shaky. So it’s not just “I get distracted and it annoys people.” It’s more like people lose confidence in me, and then my wife and I are stuck dealing with the consequences. That’s been pretty painful.

I work in computer support, so a lot of my job is following a problem step by step, like tracing a client’s network issue through logs or settings or whatever, and I’ll sit down intending to do that, but ten minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same log entries, or rereading the same few lines over and over, and I still couldn’t tell you what they actually say.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid it. I know it matters, and I’ll be looking right at the screen, but my mind just won’t stay with it long enough to get to the end. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble staying focused like this when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, at the time I don’t think anybody called it “focus” exactly, but looking back it was definitely there. My mother still jokes about it, actually — she’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” and then the rest was that I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers before I was supposed to, and I was always losing homework.\n\nAnd the annoying thing is, sometimes I had actually done the homework. It wasn’t always that I blew it off — I’d do it and then somehow it just wouldn’t make it to school, or I’d misplace it, or forget to turn it in. My mom still brings that up like it’s a funny family story, but now when I think about it, it feels a little less funny and more like, oh… this has been a pattern for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated with myself a lot, and I can feel pretty worn down by how hard everything seems to be, but “down most days”... I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m irritated or discouraged when I can’t get things done, and then I start thinking, “Why is this so hard for me?” But I don’t know if that’s the same as being depressed.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to focus, do you find that worry is what pulls your attention away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I worry after I’ve already lost focus, if that makes sense — like I’ll realize I’ve wasted time or I’m not getting anywhere, and then I start getting tense about it. But I don’t know that worry is what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time it feels more like my attention just sort of slips away without me deciding anything. I’ll be trying to read something or follow a thread of information, and then suddenly I’m somewhere else mentally. Not necessarily worrying about a specific thing, just… not with the task anymore. Then once I notice it, that’s when I get frustrated or start thinking about the consequences.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed worry being the main thing pulling me away. It’s more like the drifting happens first, and the worry comes afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep at night?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. Sometimes it’s important, and I still can’t hold onto it long enough to get through it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have these focus problems caused significant problems in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the most obvious damage has probably been professional, but it definitely spills into relationships, especially because people start to see you as unreliable.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, it’s like there isn’t that same external system holding me in place, and my reputation has taken a real hit. Yes, definitely. That’s really when it became hard to ignore.

I mean, I think I always did better when there were systems around me — schedules, other people expecting things at certain times, a clear workflow, that kind of thing. Before that, I could sort of stay on track because the job itself forced me to. There were routines and pressures built in. I mean, it’s not just work. Work is where it’s most obvious because the consequences are right in front of me, but the same thing happens in other parts of my life too.\n\nThe main thing that brought me in is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on something long enough to actually finish it anymore, even when I know it matters. Like, at work I’ll start tracing a client’s network problem, and I’m looking through logs or trying to follow the sequence of what happened, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve read the same few entries over and over and I still don’t actually know what they said. It’s like my eyes moved across the words, but my brain didn’t stay there.\n\nAnd outside of work it feels similar, just with different tasks. If I’m trying to get something done that takes sustained attention, I lose the thread. I’ll start with the intention of finishing it, and then I’m mentally gone, or I have to keep restarting because I didn’t absorb what I was doing. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. Sometimes it’s important, and I still can’t hold onto it long enough to get through it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have these focus problems caused significant problems in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the most obvious damage has probably been professional, but it definitely spills into relationships, especially because people start to see you as unreliable.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, it’s like there isn’t that same external system holding me in place, and my reputation has taken a real hit. With freelance clients, if I don’t follow through cleanly or I miss things or I’m late getting back to them, they don’t really care what the internal reason is — they just stop trusting me. I’ve lost clients that way. And that’s hard not to take personally, because I know I’m capable, but from their side it just looks like I’m not dependable.\n\nThere was also a senior service-manager role I was trying for, and I missed out on that. That one really stung, because it felt like the kind of position I should have been able to step into at this stage of my career. But when your follow-through is inconsistent, people notice.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it affects my marriage. My wife and I have had to worry about money more than we should, because if clients don’t trust me to finish things or stay on top of things, the income gets shaky. So it’s not just “I get distracted and it annoys people.” It’s more like people lose confidence in me, and then my wife and I are stuck dealing with the consequences. That’s been pretty painful.\n\nDoctor: Did your focus problems get noticeably worse after losing the structure of your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s really when it became hard to ignore.\n\nI mean, I think I always did better when there were systems around me — schedules, other people expecting things at certain times, a clear workflow, that kind of thing. But after I was fired and lost that structure, it was like the scaffolding came down. Since then, for about… I guess it’s been around a year and ten months now, it’s been causing serious trouble.\n\nBefore that, I could sort of stay on track because the job itself forced me to. There were routines and pressures built in. Once that was gone, I had to create all of that myself, and I just haven’t been able to do it consistently. That’s when the focus problems went from being frustrating to actually damaging.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often have trouble organizing tasks or managing your time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very much. Honestly, that may be one of the biggest day-to-day problems now.

When I had the old job, there was a ticketing system and an escalation calendar, and even if I didn’t love being boxed in by it, it basically told me what mattered next. There was a queue, there were priorities, there were deadlines staring at me. Without that, my days just kind of turn into… piles. Sticky notes on the desk, half-finished emails sitting open, reminders about chess club stuff, parts I need to order, messages I meant to return, things I wrote down twice because I forgot I had already written them down.

And the problem is not just that it looks messy. It’s that I honestly can’t tell what has to happen first until something is already overdue. Then suddenly it becomes obvious because someone is upset or a client is asking where something is or I realize I missed the window to order a part in time. Before that, everything feels like it’s floating around at the same level of importance, and I waste a lot of time trying to decide where to start instead of actually doing the thing.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is... I just can’t keep my mind on a task long enough to actually finish it anymore, even when it’s something important. I work in computer support, so a lot of my job is following a problem step by step, like tracing a client’s network issue through logs or settings or whatever, and I’ll sit down intending to do that, but ten minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same log entries, or rereading the same few lines over and over, and I still couldn’t tell you what they actually say.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid it. I know it matters, and I’ll be looking right at the screen, but my mind just won’t stay with it long enough to get to the end. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble staying focused like this when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, at the time I don’t think anybody called it “focus” exactly, but looking back it was definitely there. My mother still jokes about it, actually — she’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” and then the rest was that I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers before I was supposed to, and I was always losing homework.\n\nAnd the annoying thing is, sometimes I had actually done the homework. It wasn’t always that I blew it off — I’d do it and then somehow it just wouldn’t make it to school, or I’d misplace it, or forget to turn it in. My mom still brings that up like it’s a funny family story, but now when I think about it, it feels a little less funny and more like, oh… this has been a pattern for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I get frustrated with myself a lot, and I can feel pretty worn down by how hard everything seems to be, but “down most days”... I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it that way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m irritated or discouraged when I can’t get things done, and then I start thinking, “Why is this so hard for me?” But I don’t know if that’s the same as being depressed.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to focus, do you find that worry is what pulls your attention away?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I worry after I’ve already lost focus, if that makes sense — like I’ll realize I’ve wasted time or I’m not getting anywhere, and then I start getting tense about it. But I don’t know that worry is what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time it feels more like my attention just sort of slips away without me deciding anything. I’ll be trying to read something or follow a thread of information, and then suddenly I’m somewhere else mentally. Not necessarily worrying about a specific thing, just… not with the task anymore. Then once I notice it, that’s when I get frustrated or start thinking about the consequences.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed worry being the main thing pulling me away. It’s more like the drifting happens first, and the worry comes afterward.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep at night?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t really been paying close attention in a way where I could tell you, like, “Yes, it takes me exactly an hour,” or “I wake up three times.” I don’t think I’ve been tracking it that specifically.\n\nSometimes at night I’ll notice I’m still sort of mentally busy, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’d say I consistently have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep. It’s not something I’ve really thought of as the main problem, or at least I haven’t connected it clearly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I’m not sure. I’d probably have to pay more attention to it for a week or two to give you a cleaner answer.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have the same trouble staying focused in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it’s not just work. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Very easily. It’s almost embarrassing how little it takes sometimes.

If I’m working from home and I’m trying to stay with one client issue, and the dishwasher clicks or makes that little end-of-cycle sound, suddenly part of my brain is over there. Or a neighbor walks by the window and I glance up, and then I’m thinking about whether I need to move something in the driveway or whether I ever returned their message, and the thing I was doing is just… gone. Not consciously, even. I don’t decide to stop working. I just realize a few minutes later that I’ve drifted completely away from the job in front of me.

And it can be internal too. Like I’ll be looking at one machine or one support problem, and a random thought pops up about some other machine I should fix, or a part I meant to check, and before I’ve even noticed the switch happening, I’m already mentally halfway into that other problem. Then I have to drag myself back and figure out where I was, which is frustrating because it feels so automatic.

I work in computer support, so a lot of my job is following a problem step by step, like tracing a client’s network issue through logs or settings or whatever, and I’ll sit down intending to do that, but ten minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same log entries, or rereading the same few lines over and over, and I still couldn’t tell you what they actually say.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to avoid it. I know it matters, and I’ll be looking right at the screen, but my mind just won’t stay with it long enough to get to the end. That’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Did you have trouble staying focused like this when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. I mean, at the time I don’t think anybody called it “focus” exactly, but looking back it was definitely there. My mother still jokes about it, actually — she’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” and then the rest was that I wouldn’t stay in my seat, I’d blurt out answers before I was supposed to, and I was always losing homework.\n\nAnd the annoying thing is, sometimes I had actually done the homework. It wasn’t always that I blew it off — I’d do it and then somehow it just wouldn’t make it to school, or I’d misplace it, or forget to turn it in. My mom still brings that up like it’s a funny family story, but now when I think about it, it feels a little less funny and more like, oh… this has been a pattern for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Lately, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, sometimes I worry after I’ve already lost focus, if that makes sense — like I’ll realize I’ve wasted time or I’m not getting anywhere, and then I start getting tense about it. It’s not something I’ve really thought of as the main problem, or at least I haven’t connected it clearly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I’m not sure. I’d probably have to pay more attention to it for a week or two to give you a cleaner answer.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you have the same trouble staying focused in other parts of your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it’s not just work. Work is where it’s most obvious because the consequences are right in front of me, but the same thing happens in other parts of my life too.\n\nThe main thing that brought me in is that I can’t seem to keep my mind on something long enough to actually finish it anymore, even when I know it matters. Like, at work I’ll start tracing a client’s network problem, and I’m looking through logs or trying to follow the sequence of what happened, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve read the same few entries over and over and I still don’t actually know what they said. It’s like my eyes moved across the words, but my brain didn’t stay there.\n\nAnd outside of work it feels similar, just with different tasks. If I’m trying to get something done that takes sustained attention, I lose the thread. I’ll start with the intention of finishing it, and then I’m mentally gone, or I have to keep restarting because I didn’t absorb what I was doing. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. Sometimes it’s important, and I still can’t hold onto it long enough to get through it cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Have these focus problems caused significant problems in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, the most obvious damage has probably been professional, but it definitely spills into relationships, especially because people start to see you as unreliable.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, it’s like there isn’t that same external system holding me in place, and my reputation has taken a real hit. With freelance clients, if I don’t follow through cleanly or I miss things or I’m late getting back to them, they don’t really care what the internal reason is — they just stop trusting me. I’ve lost clients that way. And that’s hard not to take personally, because I know I’m capable, but from their side it just looks like I’m not dependable.\n\nThere was also a senior service-manager role I was trying for, and I missed out on that. That one really stung, because it felt like the kind of position I should have been able to step into at this stage of my career. But when your follow-through is inconsistent, people notice.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it affects my marriage. My wife and I have had to worry about money more than we should, because if clients don’t trust me to finish things or stay on top of things, the income gets shaky. So it’s not just “I get distracted and it annoys people.” It’s more like people lose confidence in me, and then my wife and I are stuck dealing with the consequences. That’s been pretty painful.\n\nDoctor: Did your focus problems get noticeably worse after losing the structure of your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That’s really when it became hard to ignore.\n\nI mean, I think I always did better when there were systems around me — schedules, other people expecting things at certain times, a clear workflow, that kind of thing. But after I was fired and lost that structure, it was like the scaffolding came down. Since then, for about… I guess it’s been around a year and ten months now, it’s been causing serious trouble.\n\nBefore that, I could sort of stay on track because the job itself forced me to. There were routines and pressures built in. Once that was gone, I had to create all of that myself, and I just haven’t been able to do it consistently. That’s when the focus problems went from being frustrating to actually damaging.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or managing your time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much. Honestly, that may be one of the biggest day-to-day problems now.\n\nWhen I had the old job, there was a ticketing system and an escalation calendar, and even if I didn’t love being boxed in by it, it basically told me what mattered next. There was a queue, there were priorities, there were deadlines staring at me. Without that, my days just kind of turn into… piles. Sticky notes on the desk, half-finished emails sitting open, reminders about chess club stuff, parts I need to order, messages I meant to return, things I wrote down twice because I forgot I had already written them down.\n\nAnd the problem is not just that it looks messy. It’s that I honestly can’t tell what has to happen first until something is already overdue. Then suddenly it becomes obvious because someone is upset or a client is asking where something is or I realize I missed the window to order a part in time. Before that, everything feels like it’s floating around at the same level of importance, and I waste a lot of time trying to decide where to start instead of actually doing the thing.\n\nDoctor: Are you easily distracted by things going on around you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very easily. It’s almost embarrassing how little it takes sometimes.\n\nIf I’m working from home and I’m trying to stay with one client issue, and the dishwasher clicks or makes that little end-of-cycle sound, suddenly part of my brain is over there. Or a neighbor walks by the window and I glance up, and then I’m thinking about whether I need to move something in the driveway or whether I ever returned their message, and the thing I was doing is just… gone. Not consciously, even. I don’t decide to stop working. I just realize a few minutes later that I’ve drifted completely away from the job in front of me.\n\nAnd it can be internal too. Like I’ll be looking at one machine or one support problem, and a random thought pops up about some other machine I should fix, or a part I meant to check, and before I’ve even noticed the switch happening, I’m already mentally halfway into that other problem. Then I have to drag myself back and figure out where I was, which is frustrating because it feels so automatic."

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I worry after I’ve already lost focus, if that makes sense — like I’ll realize I’ve wasted time or I’m not getting anywhere, and then I start getting tense about it. But I don’t know that worry is what starts it.\n\nA lot of the time it feels more like my attention just sort of slips away without me deciding anything. I’ll be trying to read something or follow a thread of information, and then suddenly I’m somewhere else mentally. Not necessarily worrying about a specific thing, just… not with the task anymore. Then once I notice it, that’s when I get frustrated or start thinking about the consequences.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I haven’t really noticed worry being the main thing pulling me away. It’s more like the drifting happens first, and the worry comes afterward.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I sleep, but I haven’t really been paying close attention in a way where I could tell you, like, “Yes, it takes me exactly an hour,” or “I wake up three times.” I don’t think I’ve been tracking it that specifically.\n\nSometimes at night I’ll notice I’m still sort of mentally busy, if that makes sense, but I don’t know if I’d say I consistently have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep. It’s not something I’ve really thought of as the main problem, or at least I haven’t connected it clearly.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say I’m not sure. I’d probably have to pay more attention to it for a week or two to give you a cleaner answer.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. It’s like my eyes moved across the words, but my brain didn’t stay there.\n\nAnd outside of work it feels similar, just with different tasks. If I’m trying to get something done that takes sustained attention, I lose the thread. I’ll start with the intention of finishing it, and then I’m mentally gone, or I have to keep restarting because I didn’t absorb what I was doing. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. Sometimes it’s important, and I still can’t hold onto it long enough to get through it cleanly.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes. I mean, the most obvious damage has probably been professional, but it definitely spills into relationships, especially because people start to see you as unreliable.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, it’s like there isn’t that same external system holding me in place, and my reputation has taken a real hit. With freelance clients, if I don’t follow through cleanly or I miss things or I’m late getting back to them, they don’t really care what the internal reason is — they just stop trusting me. I’ve lost clients that way. And that’s hard not to take personally, because I know I’m capable, but from their side it just looks like I’m not dependable.\n\nThere was also a senior service-manager role I was trying for, and I missed out on that. That one really stung, because it felt like the kind of position I should have been able to step into at this stage of my career. But when your follow-through is inconsistent, people notice.\n\nAnd at home, yeah, it affects my marriage. My wife and I have had to worry about money more than we should, because if clients don’t trust me to finish things or stay on top of things, the income gets shaky. So it’s not just “I get distracted and it annoys people.” It’s more like people lose confidence in me, and then my wife and I are stuck dealing with the consequences. That’s been pretty painful.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
